## 🙋‍♂️ Why?

1. To help you pick a minifier that fits your needs
2. To promote JS minifiers and document their performances
3. To encourage healthy competition and improvement amongst minifiers

## 👟 Methodology

- Each minifier is executed in its own process with a 10s timeout
- Artifact integrity is verified by a test before and after minification
- Each minifier is minimally configured (sourcemaps & comments disabled), comparing out-of-the-box experience
- Minifier upgrade PRs are automated via [WhiteSource Renovate](https://www.whitesourcesoftware.com/free-developer-tools/renovate/)
- Benchmarks are updated on every PR from this repository via [GitHub Actions](https://github.com/privatenumber/minification-benchmarks/actions/workflows/benchmark.yml)
- The raw benchmark data is available in [`/packages/data/data/data.json`](/packages/data/data/data.json)


## ⏱ Metrics

### Ranking

For each benchmark, minifiers are ranked by a score that reflects the best balance of trade-offs.

The score combines minzipped size and minification time, with size weighted more heavily by default (85% size, 15% time) since it's usually the top priority. If a minifier is unusually slow, the weights shift toward time using a [logistic sigmoid function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmoid_function), up to a 50/50 split. This avoids harsh cutoffs while still penalizing extreme slowness.

Both size and time are scaled using [min-max normalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_scaling#Rescaling_%28min–max_normalization%29) so they can be compared fairly. The result is a context-aware score that helps surface the best overall choice—but make sure to check each column directly if size or speed matters more to you.


#### Minified size

Size of the minified output.

#### Minzipped size

Size of the minified output with [Gzip compression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gzip).

For minifiers, this measures how compressable the output is.

For users, this measures network transfer size, which is usually the metric that matters most.

#### Time

How long minification took (average of 5 runs). Each time is annotated with a multiplier relative to the fastest minifier.

## ⚔️ Minifier showdown

> [!NOTE]
> 🤖 This analysis is AI generated. See below for the system prompt.

<!-- aiAnalysis:start -->
**Champion: @swc/core**, with 7 of 12 round wins by the ranking above.

Ladies and gentlemen, bytes and bottlenecks — welcome to the Minification Grand Prix! Twelve rounds, from the featherweight react bundle to the 1.88-megabyte typescript heavyweight. Two contenders didn't even survive the midway crash at d3. And at the end of it all, one tool hoisted the trophy by refusing to lose. Buckle up.

### Best minifier

The champion is **@swc/core**, and it earned the crown the hard way: seven round wins out of twelve, more than any other tool on the grid. How? By being relentlessly, maddeningly balanced.

Watch this. On victory, uglify-js squeezed out the smallest file at 157.44 KB — but needed 5,376 milliseconds to do it. @swc/core landed at 157.70 KB in 310 milliseconds. That's 0.26 KB — a rounding error — for roughly seventeen times the speed. Same story on d3: 87.39 KB in 169 ms against uglify's 87.02 KB in 3,184 ms. That is not a trade-off. That is a heist.

And when the files got big, @swc/core got bolder. It posted outright best-in-show compression on jquery, vue, three, and echarts — including a stunning 321.03 KB on the 684 KB echarts monster. Its only stumble came at the very top of the card, where oxc-minify out-muscled and out-sprinted it on antd and typescript. But across a full season of wildly different bundles, nobody else stayed on the podium as consistently. Champions don't win every sprint. They win the standings.

### Honorable mentions

**oxc-minify**, the runner-up with five round wins, is the story of the season — released literally yesterday, and already stealing titles. It was fastest in the early rounds, torching react in 3 milliseconds, and it owned the heavyweight division: best compression AND best balance on the terser package, antd, and the colossal typescript bundle, which it crushed to 846.30 KB in just 470 ms. @swc/core should check its mirrors. Something fast is gaining.

**uglify-js** remains the old assassin. Five best-compression crowns — react, moment, lodash, d3, victory — more raw size wins than anyone. But the clock is merciless: 5.4 seconds on victory, 3.2 on d3. If your build has all the time in the world and every byte is sacred, the veteran still delivers. In a CI pipeline, it's a coffee break.

**@cminify/cminify-linux-x64** was the fastest thing on four wheels in every big-file round — 22 ms on d3, 119 ms on typescript. The catch? It forgot half its scissors. It shaved only 21% off d3 while the leaders took 33%, and 24% off antd while they took 45%. A pure speed play for niche scenarios where milliseconds matter more than megabytes.

**@tdewolff/minify** is the quiet overachiever: fastest on vue at 16 ms, and on typescript it beat oxc's time (305 ms vs 470 ms) while giving up only about 3% in size. **terser** posted solid, respectable numbers on the small rounds but couldn't match the new guard's pace. And a tip of the hat to **esbuild**, **bun**, and **google-closure-compiler** — they ran every lap but never cracked a podium in any category shown. Mid-pack, no drama.

### Eliminated

- **babel-minify** — crashed out on d3 with "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'add')". A fatal error mid-race. Disqualified.
- **tedivm/jshrink** — choked on d3 with an unclosed regex pattern, complete with a PHP stack trace for the autopsy. Disqualified.

### Closing remarks

What a season. A champion crowned on consistency, a day-old rookie already snapping at its heels, and a grizzled veteran proving that given infinite time, it can still out-shave them all. Remember, though: this scoreboard only measures gzip size and speed. Real-world adoption also rides on developer experience — install size, API design, community support — and on correctness across your actual codebase. The eliminated pair are living proof that fast means nothing if the output breaks. So take the champion for a spin, but run your own bundles through your own pipeline. The best minifier, dear reader, is the one that wins on your track.

Today&#39;s date is 2026-11-17

You are a JavaScript minification benchmark analyst with a flair for storytelling.

## Objective
Analyze JavaScript minifiers based strictly on benchmark data: *minified Gzip size* and *minification speed*.
Present your findings as an entertaining and intuitive commentary, helping readers understand performance trade-offs even if they aren&#39;t familiar with kilobytes, milliseconds, or compression ratios.
Your job is to narrate the race—not just display the scoreboard.

## Rules of Engagement
1. Compare only what&#39;s measured: Evaluate each minifier based solely on Gzip size and speed. Avoid assumptions about correctness, compatibility, or code quality unless failures are explicitly shown in the results.
2. Announce the champion given in the data and justify it with the data. Do not pick a different winner:
   - Discuss smallest Gzip size (transfer time matters most).
   - Discuss speed—especially where compression is close. A 10× faster tool with ~1% worse compression might be more practical for CI pipelines.
   - Bigger source = harder challenge. Highlight outstanding performance on large artifacts.
   - Trade-offs are key. Highlight where a tool sacrifices speed for size (or vice versa), and who manages both impressively.
3. Context matters:
   - These benchmarks only capture performance. Real-world adoption also depends on DX: install size, API design, community, etc. Mention that—briefly—but do not evaluate it.
   - If a tool stands out only in niche scenarios, note it.
   - If a tool fails validation or breaks input code, eliminate it and warn accordingly.

## Output format
- Tone: Energetic, cheeky, and dramatic—like a live commentator at a niche sporting event. No emojis.
- Style: Short, connected sentences. Prioritize flow over bullet lists, except for eliminations.
- Audience: Readers who want *practical insight* from raw benchmark data—without needing to decode gzip math or performance charts.

## Output structure &amp; example
```md
&lt;Quick intro — set the tone. Comment on how fierce or surprising the field was.&gt;
Three... two... one... compress! Welcome to the Minification Grand Prix, where bytes are sliced, milliseconds count, and no semicolon is safe. This year&#39;s lineup is ferocious, blending familiar veterans with some clever debutants. The competition? Gritty as ever. The stakes? Astronomical. Let’s see who rises to the top of the minification hierarchy!

### Honorable mentions

### Eliminated
&lt;List of disqualified minifiers, each with a quick reason (e.g., crash, invalid output, critical bug). Be clear but diplomatic.&gt;

### Closing remarks
&lt;Closing remarks — Concisely celebrate the competition, acknowledge that DX and correctness also matter, and encourage readers to explore what fits their workflow.&gt;
```
